### Will LLMs Scaling Hit the Wall? Breaking Barriers via Distributed Resources on Massive Edge Devices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08223v1)
- **Authors**: Tao Shen, Didi Zhu, Ziyu Zhao, Chao Wu, Fei Wu
- **Abstract**: The remarkable success of foundation models has been driven by scaling laws, demonstrating that model performance improves predictably with increased training data and model size. However, this scaling trajectory faces two critical challenges: the depletion of high-quality public data, and the prohibitive computational power required for larger models, which have been monopolized by tech giants. These two bottlenecks pose significant obstacles to the further development of AI. In this position paper, we argue that leveraging massive distributed edge devices can break through these barriers. We reveal the vast untapped potential of data and computational resources on massive edge devices, and review recent technical advancements in distributed/federated learning that make this new paradigm viable. Our analysis suggests that by collaborating on edge devices, everyone can participate in training large language models with small edge devices. This paradigm shift towards distributed training on edge has the potential to democratize AI development and foster a more inclusive AI community.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Will LLMs Scaling Hit the Wall? Breaking Barriers via Distributed Resources on Massive Edge Devices" argues that the continued scaling of Large Language Models (LLMs) faces two key challenges: the depletion of high-quality public data and the concentration of computational power among a few tech giants. The authors propose a paradigm shift towards leveraging massive distributed edge devices (smartphones, IoT devices) to overcome these barriers. They highlight the vast untapped data and computational resources available on these devices, review recent advancements in distributed and federated learning, and suggest that collaborative training on edge devices can democratize AI development. The paper explores technical approaches like small language models at the edge, collaborative inference, and collaborative training, while also addressing critical challenges like heterogeneous device model fusion and compute sharing. Finally, the paper discusses the societal impact, including AI democratization, incentive mechanisms, and environmental benefits.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The idea of using edge devices for LLM training isn't entirely new. Federated learning, in general, has explored using distributed resources. However, this paper brings a needed focus specifically on **massive edge devices** as a comprehensive solution, considering both data scarcity and computational monopolization issues. The paper's strength lies in bringing together these disparate ideas into a coherent vision and argument. While individual technical components (federated learning, SLMs) are established, the *synthesis* and application to the LLM scaling problem present a degree of novelty.
The novelty of the paper is, however, limited by the fact it is mainly a position paper outlining a potential, high-level future. The paper does not provide any novel technical developments or empirical results.

**Significance:**

The significance is moderate. The challenges identified (data scarcity, compute monopolization) are indeed major concerns in the AI field. If LLM development continues on its current trajectory, these bottlenecks *will* become increasingly problematic. The potential for democratization and environmental benefits is also highly significant.
However, the paper is mostly theoretical. The practical challenges of implementing such a distributed training system are immense, including data privacy concerns, unreliable network connectivity, device heterogeneity, security vulnerabilities, and incentive design. While the paper acknowledges some of these challenges, it doesn't offer concrete solutions or demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed approach. The reliance on future advancements in edge computing and distributed algorithms also limits the current impact.
The paper's focus on the potential of edge data is a significant contribution. Highlighting the untapped resources of edge data for AI model training serves as a valuable alternative to synthetic data and public sources. The discussions of the technical challenges, are also a contribution to the discussion.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** Effectively articulates the challenges facing LLM scaling.
*   **Compelling Vision:** Presents a clear and inspiring vision for democratized AI development.
*   **Comprehensive Overview:**  Provides a good overview of related work in federated learning, small language models, and edge computing.
*   **Societal Impact Discussion:** Considers the broader societal implications of the proposed approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Technical Depth:** Doesn't offer novel technical solutions to the identified challenges.
*   **Limited Empirical Evidence:** Provides no experimental results to support the feasibility of the proposed approach.
*   **Oversimplification:** Potentially understates the complexity of implementing a truly effective and secure distributed training system on edge devices.
*   **Limited discussion of the economic feasibility.**
*   **Conservative estimation approach that may not be very reliable.**

**Potential Influence:**

The paper can influence the field by encouraging research into distributed LLM training methods on edge devices, spurring the development of new algorithms and frameworks tailored for this specific context. It can also raise awareness of the need for more democratized and environmentally sustainable AI development practices. However, its impact will depend on the subsequent development of practical solutions and empirical validations of the proposed concepts.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the paper's novelty is mainly a useful synthesis and it lacks technical depth and empirical validation, but given the identified problems are real, its contribution can still be important.

### A Grey-box Text Attack Framework using Explainable AI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08226v1)
- **Authors**: Esther Chiramal, Kelvin Soh Boon Kai
- **Abstract**: Explainable AI is a strong strategy implemented to understand complex black-box model predictions in a human interpretable language. It provides the evidence required to execute the use of trustworthy and reliable AI systems. On the other hand, however, it also opens the door to locating possible vulnerabilities in an AI model. Traditional adversarial text attack uses word substitution, data augmentation techniques and gradient-based attacks on powerful pre-trained Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers (BERT) variants to generate adversarial sentences. These attacks are generally whitebox in nature and not practical as they can be easily detected by humans E.g. Changing the word from "Poor" to "Rich". We proposed a simple yet effective Grey-box cum Black-box approach that does not require the knowledge of the model while using a set of surrogate Transformer/BERT models to perform the attack using Explainable AI techniques. As Transformers are the current state-of-the-art models for almost all Natural Language Processing (NLP) tasks, an attack generated from BERT1 is transferable to BERT2. This transferability is made possible due to the attention mechanism in the transformer that allows the model to capture long-range dependencies in a sequence. Using the power of BERT generalisation via attention, we attempt to exploit how transformers learn by attacking a few surrogate transformer variants which are all based on a different architecture. We demonstrate that this approach is highly effective to generate semantically good sentences by changing as little as one word that is not detectable by humans while still fooling other BERT models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a "grey-box" text attack framework that uses explainable AI (XAI) techniques to identify vulnerabilities in transformer-based NLP models. Instead of requiring complete knowledge of the target model's architecture or gradients (as in white-box attacks), the method uses XAI (specifically LIME) to determine the contribution of each word in a sentence to the model's prediction.  The words with the highest contribution are then replaced with synonyms, and the resulting sentences are tested against a set of "surrogate" transformer models. If an adversarial example successfully fools the surrogate models, it's considered a good candidate for attacking the target model. This approach aims to generate semantically plausible adversarial examples that are difficult for humans to detect, while still causing the model to misclassify.  The paper evaluates the framework on sentiment classification tasks, demonstrating its effectiveness in transferring attacks between different transformer architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using explainable AI to guide adversarial attacks is relatively novel. While adversarial attacks and XAI have been researched separately, the combination of them in a grey-box setup has promise. The paper builds upon existing work in adversarial attacks (e.g., TextAttack, PWWS) and XAI (LIME), but the specific way they are integrated to create a transferable attack is a valuable addition. The idea of testing adversarial sentences against multiple surrogate models to strengthen the attack before applying it to the target is another innovative aspect.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in making adversarial attacks more practical and less reliant on complete model knowledge. White-box attacks are often unrealistic because attackers rarely have access to the target model's inner workings. By using XAI and surrogate models, this framework can potentially identify vulnerabilities in deployed NLP systems without needing to reverse-engineer the target. The paper also contributes to the broader understanding of transferability in transformer models, revealing how attacks generated on one architecture can affect others due to the attention mechanisms.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practicality:** The grey-box approach makes adversarial attacks more feasible in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Interpretability:**  Leveraging XAI allows for a better understanding of *why* certain word changes lead to misclassification.
    *   **Transferability:** The framework demonstrates the ability to transfer attacks between different transformer architectures.
    *   **Clear Methodology:**  The design is well-described, and the steps are easy to follow.
    *   **Addresses an important area:** The paper addresses an important area of research by working on defense against NLP attacks, a relevant subject in the light of recent advancement in language models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation focuses primarily on a single dataset (IMDB) and a few specific transformer architectures. A more comprehensive evaluation across diverse datasets and models is needed to establish the generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Synonym Selection:** The reliance on WordNet for synonym selection may limit the effectiveness of the attack. WordNet synonyms are not always contextually appropriate, and more sophisticated synonym replacement strategies could be explored. More advanced methods like masked language models to select better synonyms may improve the results.
    *   **Scalability:** While the surrogate model approach is promising, the computational cost of evaluating adversarial examples against multiple surrogates needs to be considered. The paper doesn't deeply analyze the scalability aspect.
    *   **Lack of comparison with other gray-box techniques:** The paper does not compare the results to similar grey-box approaches in adversarial attacks, making it difficult to assess the relative performance of the proposed method.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in adversarial attacks and robustness testing of NLP models. The combination of XAI and surrogate models provides a valuable framework for identifying and mitigating vulnerabilities in NLP systems. As transformer models become increasingly prevalent, techniques for assessing their robustness and transferability are essential.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach to text adversarial attacks by combining XAI and surrogate models in a grey-box setting. This addresses the practical limitations of white-box attacks and provides insights into transferability between transformer architectures. The methodology is well-defined. The main drawback is the limited scope of the evaluation. Expanding the evaluation across more datasets, models, and synonym replacement strategies would significantly strengthen the paper and increase its impact. A comparison with existing gray-box attack methods is also needed. Despite these limitations, the paper offers a valuable contribution to the field and warrants further investigation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Aligning Text to Image in Diffusion Models is Easier Than You Think
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08250v1)
- **Authors**: Jaa-Yeon Lee, Byunghee Cha, Jeongsol Kim, Jong Chul Ye
- **Abstract**: While recent advancements in generative modeling have significantly improved text-image alignment, some residual misalignment between text and image representations still remains. Although many approaches have attempted to address this issue by fine-tuning models using various reward models, etc., we revisit the challenge from the perspective of representation alignment-an approach that has gained popularity with the success of REPresentation Alignment (REPA). We first argue that conventional text-to-image (T2I) diffusion models, typically trained on paired image and text data (i.e., positive pairs) by minimizing score matching or flow matching losses, is suboptimal from the standpoint of representation alignment. Instead, a better alignment can be achieved through contrastive learning that leverages both positive and negative pairs. To achieve this efficiently even with pretrained models, we introduce a lightweight contrastive fine tuning strategy called SoftREPA that uses soft text tokens. This approach improves alignment with minimal computational overhead by adding fewer than 1M trainable parameters to the pretrained model. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that our method explicitly increases the mutual information between text and image representations, leading to enhanced semantic consistency. Experimental results across text-to-image generation and text-guided image editing tasks validate the effectiveness of our approach in improving the semantic consistency of T2I generative models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Aligning Text to Image in Diffusion Models is Easier Than You Think":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SoftREPA, a lightweight contrastive fine-tuning strategy designed to improve text-image alignment in diffusion models. The core idea is to use learnable "soft text tokens" appended to text features during the diffusion process.  These tokens are trained via a contrastive loss, encouraging better alignment between text and image representations. The authors argue that standard T2I diffusion training, which primarily focuses on positive text-image pairs, is suboptimal for representation alignment.  SoftREPA improves alignment with minimal computational overhead, adding a small number of trainable parameters to pre-trained models.  The authors theoretically show that their approach increases mutual information between text and image representations, leading to enhanced semantic consistency. Experimental results across text-to-image generation and text-guided image editing validate the method's effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using contrastive learning to improve text-image alignment in diffusion models is not entirely new, as the authors themselves acknowledge existing work in representation alignment. However, the specific implementation using "soft text tokens" trained with a contrastive loss and the focus on a *minimal* parameter addition is a valuable contribution. The paper carefully reframes the problem, arguing that the standard training procedure for diffusion models isn't optimized for representation *alignment*. The novelty lies in the combination of contrastive learning, soft tokens, and a focus on parameter efficiency within the context of pre-trained diffusion models.

*   **Significance:** Improving text-image alignment is a significant goal for diffusion models, directly impacting the quality and controllability of generated images. SoftREPA's lightweight nature is a notable advantage, as it enables fine-tuning without significant computational burden. The results on various text-to-image tasks, particularly in image editing, showcase the practical benefits of the approach. The theoretical justification based on mutual information adds weight to the empirical findings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Parameter Efficiency:** The method's key selling point is its minimal computational overhead.
    *   **Flexibility:** SoftREPA can be applied to different pre-trained diffusion models (SD1.5, SDXL, SD3), demonstrating its general applicability.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The analysis linking SoftREPA to mutual information provides a strong theoretical justification for the method.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper evaluates SoftREPA on both image generation and editing tasks, providing a thorough assessment of its capabilities.
    *   The Ablation study demonstrates the importance of the key parameters in the model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are positive, the improvements over baseline methods aren't always substantial. The qualitative examples sometimes reveal subtle differences.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Contrastive Learning:** The core concept of using contrastive learning for multimodal alignment is not groundbreaking. The paper refines and adapts this concept to the specific context of diffusion models.

*   **Potential Influence:** SoftREPA has the potential to influence future research on text-image diffusion models by demonstrating the effectiveness of lightweight representation alignment strategies. Its simplicity and parameter efficiency make it an attractive option for researchers and practitioners looking to improve text-image consistency without substantial retraining or fine-tuning costs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-motivated, theoretically sound, and empirically validated method for improving text-image alignment in diffusion models. While the core idea of contrastive learning is not entirely novel, the specific implementation using soft tokens and the emphasis on parameter efficiency make a useful contribution to the field. The thorough experimental evaluation and theoretical analysis strengthen the paper's credibility. However, the sometimes incremental nature of the improvements prevents a higher score.

### SARA: Structural and Adversarial Representation Alignment for Training-efficient Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08253v1)
- **Authors**: Hesen Chen, Junyan Wang, Zhiyu Tan, Hao Li
- **Abstract**: Modern diffusion models encounter a fundamental trade-off between training efficiency and generation quality. While existing representation alignment methods, such as REPA, accelerate convergence through patch-wise alignment, they often fail to capture structural relationships within visual representations and ensure global distribution consistency between pretrained encoders and denoising networks. To address these limitations, we introduce SARA, a hierarchical alignment framework that enforces multi-level representation constraints: (1) patch-wise alignment to preserve local semantic details, (2) autocorrelation matrix alignment to maintain structural consistency within representations, and (3) adversarial distribution alignment to mitigate global representation discrepancies. Unlike previous approaches, SARA explicitly models both intra-representation correlations via self-similarity matrices and inter-distribution coherence via adversarial alignment, enabling comprehensive alignment across local and global scales. Experiments on ImageNet-256 show that SARA achieves an FID of 1.36 while converging twice as fast as REPA, surpassing recent state-of-the-art image generation methods. This work establishes a systematic paradigm for optimizing diffusion training through hierarchical representation alignment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SARA: Structural and Adversarial Representation Alignment for Training-efficient Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SARA, a hierarchical alignment framework for training diffusion models more efficiently. SARA addresses limitations in existing representation alignment methods like REPA by incorporating three complementary levels of alignment: (1) patch-wise alignment for local semantic details, (2) structural autocorrelation matrix alignment to maintain structural consistency within representations, and (3) adversarial distribution alignment to enforce global distribution consistency between the pre-trained encoder and the denoising network. SARA explicitly models intra-representation correlations and inter-distribution coherence. Experiments on ImageNet-256 show SARA achieves a better FID score and converges faster than REPA, surpassing recent state-of-the-art image generation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its hierarchical approach to representation alignment.  While REPA focused primarily on patch-wise alignment, SARA adds structural and adversarial alignment components. The specific implementations of the structural autocorrelation matrix alignment and the GAN-based adversarial distribution alignment appear to be novel contributions. The combination of these three distinct alignment strategies within a single framework is also a significant innovative aspect. However, each component alone is not groundbreaking. Autocorrelation matrices and GAN-based alignment are standard techniques in other contexts, and the novelty lies in their application to diffusion model representation alignment.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper rests on its ability to improve the training efficiency and generation quality of diffusion models.  The experiments demonstrate a faster convergence rate and better FID scores compared to REPA and other established methods. This improvement in efficiency is a major benefit, given the computational cost of training large diffusion models.  The systematic hierarchical approach suggests a promising paradigm for future research and optimization of diffusion model training. It provides an elegant solution for bridging the gap between the pre-trained encoder and diffusion network by ensuring better semantic consistency and higher generation quality. A notable strength of the paper is the extensive empirical analysis that supports the effectiveness of each proposed component.
    However, the experiments are primarily limited to ImageNet-256. Testing on more diverse and complex datasets would strengthen the claims of general applicability. Moreover, while the 2x speed up compared to REPA is significant, the absolute training time improvements need to be considered in the context of total training costs to fully assess practical benefits.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of patch-wise alignment and motivates the need for a more comprehensive approach.
    *   **Well-defined framework:** SARA provides a structured and modular framework for representation alignment.
    *   **Empirical validation:** Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of SARA compared to existing methods.
    *   **Ablation studies:** Ablation studies confirm the contribution of each component of the framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset diversity:** The experiments are primarily focused on ImageNet-256, which may not fully represent the complexity of real-world image generation tasks. Testing on larger and more complex datasets would significantly enhance the results of the research.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the complete hierarchical architecture is novel, some components (e.g., adversarial alignment using GANs) are individually less novel.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper does not delve into the detailed computational cost implications of SARA. It is essential to consider that the computational price required to establish better alignment and the overall practicality of training when determining overall efficiency increases.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-validated framework for representation alignment in diffusion models. The hierarchical approach, combining patch-wise, structural, and adversarial alignment, demonstrably improves training efficiency and generation quality. While the individual components aren't groundbreaking and some experimental validation is limited, the integrated system showcases a significant contribution. The potential impact on reducing the training costs of diffusion models, together with the systematic nature of the approach, justifies a positive evaluation. However, the limitations in dataset diversity and detailed cost analysis prevent a higher score.

### LangTime: A Language-Guided Unified Model for Time Series Forecasting with Proximal Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08271v1)
- **Authors**: Wenzhe Niu, Zongxia Xie, Yanru Sun, Wei He, Man Xu, Chao Hao
- **Abstract**: Recent research has shown an increasing interest in utilizing pre-trained large language models (LLMs) for a variety of time series applications. However, there are three main challenges when using LLMs as foundational models for time series forecasting: (1) Cross-domain generalization. (2) Cross-modality alignment. (3) Error accumulation in autoregressive frameworks. To address these challenges, we proposed LangTime, a language-guided unified model for time series forecasting that incorporates cross-domain pre-training with reinforcement learning-based fine-tuning. Specifically, LangTime constructs Temporal Comprehension Prompts (TCPs), which include dataset-wise and channel-wise instructions, to facilitate domain adaptation and condense time series into a single token, enabling LLMs to understand better and align temporal data. To improve autoregressive forecasting, we introduce TimePPO, a reinforcement learning-based fine-tuning algorithm. TimePPO mitigates error accumulation by leveraging a multidimensional rewards function tailored for time series and a repeat-based value estimation strategy. Extensive experiments demonstrate that LangTime achieves state-of-the-art cross-domain forecasting performance, while TimePPO fine-tuning effectively enhances the stability and accuracy of autoregressive forecasting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LangTime: A Language-Guided Unified Model for Time Series Forecasting with Proximal Policy Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LangTime, a novel language-guided model for time series forecasting that addresses three key challenges: cross-domain generalization, cross-modality alignment, and error accumulation in autoregressive frameworks. LangTime employs Temporal Comprehension Prompts (TCPs) to provide domain and channel-specific information to a pre-trained large language model (LLM). This helps LLMs understand and align with time series data. To mitigate error accumulation during forecasting, the authors introduce TimePPO, a reinforcement learning-based fine-tuning algorithm built upon Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). TimePPO uses a tailored reward function and a repeat-based value estimation strategy.  Experimental results on various benchmark datasets demonstrate that LangTime achieves state-of-the-art cross-domain forecasting performance and that TimePPO enhances the stability and accuracy of autoregressive forecasting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to time series forecasting by effectively integrating LLMs. While using LLMs for time series isn't entirely new, the combination of Temporal Comprehension Prompts and the TimePPO fine-tuning algorithm provides a unique solution to address the specific challenges in the domain. The TCPs are a clever way to bridge the modality gap. The TimePPO adaptation for autoregressive time series forecasting is a significant contribution. The repeat-based value estimation is a good idea, but its actual improvement on established autoregressive methods is what really matters and is substantiated by results.
*   **Significance:**  The paper tackles a significant problem – improving the generalization and accuracy of time series forecasting. Successfully leveraging the power of LLMs for this task could have a considerable impact on various applications, from finance to energy management and weather prediction. The experimental results demonstrate a noticeable improvement over existing methods, supporting the paper's claims. However, the extent of improvement might be dataset-specific, which requires further investigation. The zero-shot results are notable and suggest genuine progress toward generalization.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The problem formulation is clear and well-motivated.
    *   The proposed LangTime model is well-designed, with a strong rationale for each component.
    *   The introduction of TCPs demonstrates creativity in aligning LLMs with time series data.
    *   The adaptation of PPO (TimePPO) effectively addresses the error accumulation problem in autoregressive models, which is one of the most common sources of frustration when using autoregressive methods.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough, covering various datasets and comparing against state-of-the-art baselines.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of different components.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The computational cost of TimePPO compared to standard supervised fine-tuning could be a concern. While the paper mentions simplification, it lacks a direct comparison of computational resources and time.
    *   While the paper demonstrates good zero-shot performance, its performance still lags behind domain-specific forecasting methods that use time series analysis, but are limited to being effective in their defined fields.
    *   Some of the comparative baselines (GPT4TS) have been criticized for having poor implementations and limited applicability to specific fields and conditions. If other methods are also limited, this should be noted and clarified in the comparisons.
    *   The datasets used for pretraining are publicly available, but limited. The model might benefit from pretraining on a larger dataset that more closely represents the diversity of real-world time series data.

*   **Potential Impact:**  LangTime's approach could influence future research in time series forecasting by inspiring further investigation into language-guided models and reinforcement learning-based fine-tuning techniques. The work could also encourage the development of more robust and generalizable time series models. The open-source code will allow others to build upon this work.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed approach with reasonable novelty and significant results. The TCPs and TimePPO contributions address specific challenges in applying LLMs to time series data, and the experimental evaluation supports the claims. However, some limitations, such as computational cost and reliance on limited pre-training data, prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The strength and success of TimePPO, compared to other autoregressive methods, is the biggest contribution. Furthermore, the model has been tested, but not proven robust in various and specialized fields. However, this paper does show the potential that time series analysis has when coupled with NLP.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### PromptLNet: Region-Adaptive Aesthetic Enhancement via Prompt Guidance in Low-Light Enhancement Net
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08276v1)
- **Authors**: Jun Yin, Yangfan He, Miao Zhang, Pengyu Zeng, Tianyi Wang, Shuai Lu, Xueqian Wang
- **Abstract**: Learning and improving large language models through human preference feedback has become a mainstream approach, but it has rarely been applied to the field of low-light image enhancement. Existing low-light enhancement evaluations typically rely on objective metrics (such as FID, PSNR, etc.), which often result in models that perform well objectively but lack aesthetic quality. Moreover, most low-light enhancement models are primarily designed for global brightening, lacking detailed refinement. Therefore, the generated images often require additional local adjustments, leading to research gaps in practical applications. To bridge this gap, we propose the following innovations: 1) We collect human aesthetic evaluation text pairs and aesthetic scores from multiple low-light image datasets (e.g., LOL, LOL2, LOM, DCIM, MEF, etc.) to train a low-light image aesthetic evaluation model, supplemented by an optimization algorithm designed to fine-tune the diffusion model. 2) We propose a prompt-driven brightness adjustment module capable of performing fine-grained brightness and aesthetic adjustments for specific instances or regions. 3) We evaluate our method alongside existing state-of-the-art algorithms on mainstream benchmarks. Experimental results show that our method not only outperforms traditional methods in terms of visual quality but also provides greater flexibility and controllability, paving the way for improved aesthetic quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PromptLNet, a novel framework for region-adaptive aesthetic enhancement of low-light images using prompt guidance. It addresses the limitations of existing low-light enhancement methods, which often lack aesthetic quality and fine-grained control.  PromptLNet incorporates several key innovations: (1) a new collected human aesthetic evaluation dataset and model tailored for low-light images, (2) a prompt-driven brightness adjustment module for localized and aesthetic refinement, and (3) Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) to better align the model's output with human preferences. The framework combines a Retinex-based Reasoning Segment (RRS) module for target localization, a Brightness Controllable (BC) module for precise lighting adjustments, and an Adaptive Contextual Compensation (ACC) module for context-aware modifications within a diffusion model.  The approach allows users to adjust brightness and aesthetics using natural language prompts. Experimental results demonstrate that PromptLNet outperforms existing state-of-the-art algorithms in both visual quality and controllability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in the combination of several components and the application of RLHF to low-light image enhancement with prompt control.
    *   **Human Aesthetic Evaluation:** The collection of an aesthetic evaluation dataset specifically for low-light images is a positive contribution, as existing objective metrics are often insufficient for evaluating aesthetic quality in this domain. The use of RLHF to fine-tune a diffusion model based on this dataset is a strong point.
    *   **Prompt-Driven Adjustment:** The prompt-driven brightness adjustment module, enabling users to control enhancement via natural language, is another novel aspect. The combination of language models, Retinex theory, and spatial attention is well-motivated.
    *   **System Integration:** The architecture, combining RRS, BC, and ACC modules within a diffusion framework, is a relatively novel integration of existing and modified components for this specific task. The use of ControlNet to control the diffusion process based on the language prompts is a solid approach.

* **Significance:** The significance of the work is that it offers a more controllable and aesthetically pleasing approach to low-light image enhancement.  The potential impact includes:
    *   **Improved Aesthetic Quality:** By incorporating human feedback through RLHF, the generated images are more likely to align with user preferences compared to methods relying solely on objective metrics.
    *   **Enhanced Controllability:** The prompt-driven interface provides users with more intuitive and flexible control over the enhancement process, enabling region-specific adjustments and tailored results.
    *   **Practical Applications:** The method has the potential to improve various applications, such as photography, surveillance, and autonomous driving, by generating clearer and more aesthetically pleasing images in low-light conditions.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Well-motivated and clearly presented problem definition.
    *   Novel combination of existing techniques (diffusion models, language models, RLHF) tailored for low-light enhancement.
    *   Strong experimental results demonstrating improved performance in terms of both visual quality and controllability.
    *   Collection and use of a novel dataset for low-light image aesthetic evaluation.
    *   Ablation studies to validate the contribution of each module.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper describes the components well, a deeper analysis of failure cases (when and why the prompt-driven method fails) would strengthen the paper.
    *   The novelty, while present in the integration and application, relies on existing components. The individual modules are not entirely revolutionary.
    *   The quantitative comparisons could be expanded to include a wider range of datasets and metrics.  A more thorough analysis of the computational complexity of the method would also be beneficial.
    *   The paper could delve deeper into the limitations of the current aesthetic reward evaluator and potential biases it may introduce. The subjective nature of aesthetics and the potential for annotator disagreement could also be discussed.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in low-light image enhancement, particularly in the areas of controllable enhancement, aesthetic evaluation, and the application of RLHF. The prompt-driven interface and the focus on human preferences offer a promising direction for future development.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a significant and novel approach to low-light image enhancement by integrating RLHF with prompt-driven control.  While the individual components are not entirely new, their combination and application to this specific problem are well-motivated and lead to strong experimental results. The contribution of the paper is the system-level innovation, showing how different pieces can be combined to provide a better solution.  The paper would be significantly strengthened by more thorough error analysis, a discussion of the limitations of the aesthetic reward evaluator, and more diverse quantitative comparisons. However, the presented work is a clear and noteworthy contribution to the field of image enhancement.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### OminiControl2: Efficient Conditioning for Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08280v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenxiong Tan, Qiaochu Xue, Xingyi Yang, Songhua Liu, Xinchao Wang
- **Abstract**: Fine-grained control of text-to-image diffusion transformer models (DiT) remains a critical challenge for practical deployment. While recent advances such as OminiControl and others have enabled a controllable generation of diverse control signals, these methods face significant computational inefficiency when handling long conditional inputs. We present OminiControl2, an efficient framework that achieves efficient image-conditional image generation. OminiControl2 introduces two key innovations: (1) a dynamic compression strategy that streamlines conditional inputs by preserving only the most semantically relevant tokens during generation, and (2) a conditional feature reuse mechanism that computes condition token features only once and reuses them across denoising steps. These architectural improvements preserve the original framework's parameter efficiency and multi-modal versatility while dramatically reducing computational costs. Our experiments demonstrate that OminiControl2 reduces conditional processing overhead by over 90% compared to its predecessor, achieving an overall 5.9$\times$ speedup in multi-conditional generation scenarios. This efficiency enables the practical implementation of complex, multi-modal control for high-quality image synthesis with DiT models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OminiControl2: Efficient Conditioning for Diffusion Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OminiControl2, an efficient framework designed to enhance the computational performance of Diffusion Transformer (DiT) models, specifically when dealing with multiple or lengthy conditional inputs. OminiControl2 builds upon the original OminiControl framework and addresses its computational bottlenecks by employing two key innovations:

1.  **Dynamic Compression Strategy:** This reduces the sequence length of conditional inputs by selectively retaining only the most semantically relevant tokens. This includes downsampling conditional images and pruning non-informative tokens.

2.  **Conditional Feature Reuse Mechanism:** This computes conditional token features only once during the initial inference step and reuses them across subsequent denoising steps, eliminating redundant computations.

The authors demonstrate that OminiControl2 retains the parameter efficiency and versatile control capabilities of its predecessor while achieving significant reductions in computational costs. Experiments show a substantial decrease in conditional processing overhead and a significant speedup in multi-conditional generation scenarios, enabling practical applications for complex control tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination and application of techniques to address the computational challenges of conditioning DiTs with multiple inputs. While the individual components (token compression, feature reuse) are not entirely novel on their own (as noted by referencing work on caching tokens and model pruning), the way they are tailored and integrated into the OminiControl framework for efficient, multi-modal DiT control is original. The asymmetric attention mask, while conceptually simple, also contributes a novel adaptation for feature reuse in a DiT context.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical practical limitation of DiTs: their high computational cost, especially when dealing with complex conditions. By providing a framework for significantly reducing this cost, OminiControl2 directly increases the feasibility of using DiTs in real-world applications where multiple control modalities are required. The demonstrated speedups are impressive. The method is also valuable since it can be combined with other acceleration techniques such as model pruning.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the computational bottlenecks of existing controllable DiT frameworks.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The proposed techniques are clearly explained and justified with both theoretical arguments and empirical analysis (cosine similarity).
*   **Strong Empirical Validation:**  The experiments demonstrate substantial performance improvements across various conditioning tasks and configurations, using standard metrics. The ablation studies clearly illustrate the contribution of each component.
*   **Good Baselines:** The use of existing methods for both controllable generation and diffusion transformer acceleration as baselines to illustrate performance.
*   **Preservation of Quality:** The paper explicitly addresses and demonstrates the preservation of image quality while optimizing for efficiency.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The focus is heavily on computational efficiency, and while image quality is maintained, there is no significant leap in generation quality or control granularity. The method builds on existing methods, which limits potential breakthroughs.
*   **Incremental Improvements:** The approach is largely incremental, relying on existing principles and adapted strategies rather than introducing revolutionary concepts.
*   **Task-Specific tuning:** While the authors state parameter-efficiency, the methods for achieving best performance are dependent on the task. For example, selecting what tokens to prune or parameters for downsampling could be difficult in a real-world deployment.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5** to this paper. Here's the rationale:

*   It effectively addresses a practical challenge in DiT deployment, making controllable generation more accessible.
*   The methods are well-explained, empirically validated, and demonstrate clear performance gains.
*   The integration of token pruning and feature reuse are both novel ideas within the DiT setting.

However, the core ideas of token compression and feature reuse are not fundamentally new, and the improvements are incremental rather than revolutionary. While the focus on *efficiency* is valuable, the absence of significant advances in *generation quality* or *novel control mechanisms* prevents a higher score.  It is also a bit too focused on computational efficiency at the expense of other aspects such as improved control granularity or novel editing capabilities, and while it builds upon OminiControl, it also depends on the limitations of it.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Outpatient Referral: Problem Definition, Benchmarking and Challenges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08292v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoxiao Liu, Qingying Xiao, Junying Chen, Xiangyi Feng, Xiangbo Wu, Bairui Zhang, Xiang Wan, Jian Chang, Guangjun Yu, Yan Hu, Benyou Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to outpatient referral tasks across healthcare systems. However, there is a lack of standardized evaluation criteria to assess their effectiveness, particularly in dynamic, interactive scenarios. In this study, we systematically examine the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in managing tasks within Intelligent Outpatient Referral (IOR) systems and propose a comprehensive evaluation framework specifically designed for such systems. This framework comprises two core tasks: static evaluation, which focuses on evaluating the ability of predefined outpatient referrals, and dynamic evaluation, which evaluates capabilities of refining outpatient referral recommendations through iterative dialogues. Our findings suggest that LLMs offer limited advantages over BERT-like models, but show promise in asking effective questions during interactive dialogues.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the task of outpatient referral in healthcare systems, formulating it as both a static classification problem (matching patient information to a department) and a dynamic questioning problem (refining referrals through interactive dialogues). The authors introduce a novel Intelligent Outpatient Referral (IOR) benchmark consisting of 1,476 cases to evaluate LLMs on these two tasks. They propose both static and dynamic evaluation methods.  The findings suggest that LLMs offer limited advantages over BERT-like models in static classification but show promise in asking effective questions during interactive dialogues.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   **Strength:** The introduction of the IOR-Bench is a key novel aspect. Existing benchmarks often lack the dynamic, interactive element crucial for real-world outpatient referral scenarios. The formulation of the problem into distinct static and dynamic components is also a valuable contribution.
    *   **Weakness:** While the benchmark is novel, the individual components (static classification and dialogue evaluation) are not entirely new concepts in NLP. The static evaluation closely resembles typical text classification tasks. The core methodology of evaluating models in a simulated interactive dialogue builds on prior work, although the application to outpatient referral is the distinguishing factor.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Strength:** Outpatient referral is a significant real-world problem within healthcare, particularly in systems where patients directly access specialists.  Improving the accuracy and efficiency of this process can have tangible benefits in terms of patient flow, waiting times, and resource allocation. The study's focus on LLMs in this context is timely, given the growing interest in applying LLMs to healthcare.
    *   **Weakness:** The paper's conclusion that LLMs provide limited advantages over BERT-like models in static classification may limit its immediate practical significance. While the promise in dynamic questioning is noted, the challenges and inconsistencies in follow-up question generation diminish the overall impact. The evaluation setup is constrained to simulated dialogues and doesn't address the complexities of real-world patient interactions (e.g., handling diverse accents, emotional states, or non-cooperative patients). The scale and diversity of the dataset, while useful, might not fully capture the nuances of medical consultations.

*   **Rigor:**
    *   **Strength:** The paper systematically defines the problem, proposes appropriate evaluation metrics, and compares multiple LLMs with a traditional classification model (BERT). The inclusion of human baseline evaluations provides a valuable point of comparison.
    *   **Weakness:** The details of the data pre-processing steps (e.g., anonymization) could be more transparent. Further analysis of the types of errors made by the LLMs and human experts would provide deeper insights. The reliance on automatic evaluation metrics (accuracy) may not fully capture the quality of the generated dialogues, and further consideration should be given to metrics such as relevance and coherence.
*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   **Strength:** The IOR-Bench could serve as a valuable resource for future research in this area. It provides a concrete task definition, dataset, and evaluation methodology for comparing different approaches. The paper's findings highlight the need for further research into how LLMs can be effectively used for dynamic questioning and interactive decision-making in healthcare.
    *   **Weakness:** If the IOR-Bench and the findings do not inspire significant advancements or adoption by other research groups, the impact will be less pronounced.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel benchmark for a relevant and important problem in healthcare. It methodically evaluates LLMs in both static and dynamic settings, highlighting strengths and weaknesses. However, the limited advantages of LLMs in the static classification task, alongside the challenges in dynamic questioning, temper the paper's immediate practical significance. While the IOR-Bench has the potential to influence future research, its long-term impact remains to be seen. The limitations of the evaluation framework also impact the score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### D3PO: Preference-Based Alignment of Discrete Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08295v1)
- **Authors**: Umberto Borso, Davide Paglieri, Jude Wells, Tim Rocktäschel
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have achieved state-of-the-art performance across multiple domains, with recent advancements extending their applicability to discrete data. However, aligning discrete diffusion models with task-specific preferences remains challenging, particularly in scenarios where explicit reward functions are unavailable. In this work, we introduce Discrete Diffusion DPO (D3PO), the first adaptation of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) to discrete diffusion models formulated as continuous-time Markov chains. Our approach derives a novel loss function that directly fine-tunes the generative process using preference data while preserving fidelity to a reference distribution. We validate D3PO on a structured binary sequence generation task, demonstrating that the method effectively aligns model outputs with preferences while maintaining structural validity. Our results highlight that D3PO enables controlled fine-tuning without requiring explicit reward models, making it a practical alternative to reinforcement learning-based approaches. Future research will explore extending D3PO to more complex generative tasks, including language modeling and protein sequence generation, as well as investigating alternative noise schedules, such as uniform noising, to enhance flexibility across different applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Discrete Diffusion DPO (D3PO), a novel adaptation of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) for discrete diffusion models. Unlike continuous diffusion models that rely on score-matching, discrete diffusion models are formulated as Continuous-Time Markov Chains (CTMCs). The authors derive a new loss function that directly fine-tunes the generative process of a CTMC-based discrete diffusion model using pairwise preference data while maintaining fidelity to a reference distribution. The method is validated on a structured binary sequence generation task, demonstrating its ability to align model outputs with preferences while preserving structural validity, without requiring an explicit reward model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of adapting DPO to discrete diffusion models is a significant contribution. Existing DPO methods are primarily geared towards autoregressive and continuous generative models, and the shift to CTMCs necessitates a different optimization framework. The derivation of the new loss function tailored for CTMC formulations represents a key technical novelty. The simplification of the objective under a masking-state noising process and the theoretical insights gained are also valuable additions.

*   **Significance:** D3PO has the potential to significantly impact the field of discrete sequence generation. Its ability to align discrete diffusion models with task-specific preferences, especially when explicit reward functions are unavailable, addresses a key challenge. This makes controlled fine-tuning of discrete diffusion models more practical and accessible, reducing the reliance on reinforcement learning-based approaches or hand-crafted reward functions. The demonstrated ability to maintain distributional coherence while aligning with preferences is also an important feature.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Rigorous derivation of the loss function for discrete diffusion models within the CTMC framework.
    *   Theoretical insights from simplifying the objective under a masking-state noising process.
    *   Empirical validation showing alignment with preferences and preservation of structural validity.
    *   Elimination of the need for explicit reward modeling, making fine-tuning more practical.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The empirical validation is limited to a relatively simple structured binary sequence generation task. While the results are promising, the generalizability of D3PO to more complex tasks such as language modeling or protein sequence generation needs further investigation.
    *   The paper lacks a detailed comparison with existing methods for fine-tuning discrete diffusion models, such as those based on reinforcement learning or guidance techniques. A thorough benchmarking would strengthen the claims of the paper.
    *   The paper would benefit from an ablation study to understand the impact of different components of the D3PO loss function.

*   **Potential Impact:** The introduction of D3PO could stimulate further research in preference-based alignment of discrete generative models. The method's ability to align with human preferences without needing to model a reward signal can democratize the process of fine-tuning generative models and can benefit domains such as biological sequence design.

* **Over claimed novelty**
The method is a solid and sound application of an existing preference-based optimization approach to a new problem setting. However, while D3PO tailors DPO for CTMC, it primarily involves loss function derivation within this specific framework. This adaptation is valuable, but it is not a fundamental leap in preference optimization theory itself. It solves a problem that is naturally arising due to the progress of diffusion models being applied to the discrete domain.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. D3PO represents a substantial step forward in preference alignment of discrete diffusion models and addresses a significant practical challenge. The detailed derivations of the specialized loss function are valuable. The study would benefit from greater experimentation as well as ablation/sensitivity studies that would further strengthen the contribution. Finally, I feel the novelty of the idea might be inflated, and that a score higher than 7, would be an overestimation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Model as Meta-Surrogate for Data-Driven Many-Task Optimization: A Proof-of-Principle Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08301v1)
- **Authors**: Xian-Rong Zhang, Yue-Jiao Gong, Jun Zhang
- **Abstract**: In many-task optimization scenarios, surrogate models are valuable for mitigating the computational burden of repeated fitness evaluations across tasks. This study proposes a novel meta-surrogate framework to assist many-task optimization, by leveraging the knowledge transfer strengths and emergent capabilities of large language models (LLMs). We formulate a unified framework for many-task fitness prediction, by defining a universal model with metadata to fit a group of problems. Fitness prediction is performed on metadata and decision variables, enabling efficient knowledge sharing across tasks and adaptability to new tasks. The LLM-based meta-surrogate treats fitness prediction as conditional probability estimation, employing a unified token sequence representation for task metadata, inputs, and outputs. This approach facilitates efficient inter-task knowledge sharing through shared token embeddings and captures complex task dependencies via multi-task model training. Experimental results demonstrate the model's emergent generalization ability, including zero-shot performance on problems with unseen dimensions. When integrated into evolutionary transfer optimization (ETO), our framework supports dual-level knowledge transfer -- at both the surrogate and individual levels -- enhancing optimization efficiency and robustness. This work establishes a novel foundation for applying LLMs in surrogate modeling, offering a versatile solution for many-task optimization.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel meta-surrogate framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) for data-driven many-task optimization. The core idea is to fine-tune an LLM to predict fitness values based on a unified token sequence representation of task metadata, decision variables, and the output fitness.  This approach enables knowledge sharing across tasks and adaptability to new tasks, even those with unseen dimensions (zero-shot learning). The LLM-based surrogate is integrated into evolutionary transfer optimization (ETO) algorithms.  The paper presents a proof-of-principle study, demonstrating the model's generalization ability and its effectiveness in enhancing optimization efficiency and robustness within the ETO framework. The paper validates the framework with experiments performed using BBOB test functions integrated with both MaTDE and BLKT-DE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core concept of using LLMs as meta-surrogates in optimization is novel. Previous surrogate-assisted optimization and ETO research has primarily relied on traditional machine learning models.  The use of a unified token-based representation for problem metadata, inputs, and outputs to train a single LLM across multiple tasks is also a significant contribution. This provides a potentially more powerful way to perform knowledge transfer in ETO compared to existing methods that train surrogates in isolation.

**Significance:** The potential impact of this work could be substantial, especially in expensive optimization scenarios where function evaluations are costly.  The ability to leverage pre-trained LLMs and achieve zero-shot performance on unseen dimensions reduces the data requirements for new tasks.  This translates to a significant reduction in the computational budget required for optimization, making complex real-world problems more tractable. Moreover, the demonstrated integration with existing ETO algorithms makes the approach readily deployable. This paves the way for the next generation of offline evolutionary optimizers that leverage large language models.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty of Approach:** Combining LLMs and ETO in this particular way is a fresh take on surrogate-assisted optimization.
*   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the many-task fitness prediction problem.
*   **Sound Methodology:**  The design of the token sequence representation, the training approach (prefix tuning, priority-aware weighted cross-entropy), and the experimental setup are well-explained and technically sound.
*   **Promising Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate that the LLM-based meta-surrogate can achieve competitive performance compared to traditional surrogates (RBFN, GP, MLP) while requiring less data. Demonstrating some generalization ability to tasks with different dimensionalities (zero-shot) is a strong result.
* **Well-written and well-structured:** The paper is easy to understand and well written.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Proof-of-Principle Study:** The current study is primarily a proof-of-concept and needs further validation on a wider range of more complex and real-world problems.  The BBOB functions, while useful for benchmarking, are not representative of all types of optimization landscapes.
*   **Limited Metadata:** The metadata used in the experiments is relatively simple (function name, dimensionality).  Real-world problems often involve more complex and nuanced metadata, and it remains unclear how well the LLM-based surrogate would scale to such scenarios.
*   **Ablation Study:** It's not sufficiently demonstrated how much each specific component of the architecture contributes to the overall performance. For example, the significance of PWCE is not explicitly addressed, but only indirectly with different methods of token encoding.
* **Limited LLM Comparison:** Using T5 to demonstrate this meta-surrogate approach is sufficient as a proof-of-concept, it would be relevant to extend this investigation for larger LLMs such as GPT-4 or DeepSeek.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of surrogate-assisted optimization and ETO by introducing a new and potentially powerful approach based on LLMs. The experimental results are encouraging, showing that the LLM-based meta-surrogate can achieve competitive performance and demonstrates some zero-shot generalization ability. However, the study's proof-of-principle nature and some methodological limitations (such as relatively simple metadata and using T5) suggest that further research is needed to fully realize the potential of this approach. Therefore, the paper deserves a score that reflects its novelty and promise, but also acknowledges the need for further validation and development.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### General-Purpose Aerial Intelligent Agents Empowered by Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08302v1)
- **Authors**: Ji Zhao, Xiao Lin
- **Abstract**: The emergence of large language models (LLMs) opens new frontiers for unmanned aerial vehicle (UAVs), yet existing systems remain confined to predefined tasks due to hardware-software co-design challenges. This paper presents the first aerial intelligent agent capable of open-world task execution through tight integration of LLM-based reasoning and robotic autonomy. Our hardware-software co-designed system addresses two fundamental limitations: (1) Onboard LLM operation via an edge-optimized computing platform, achieving 5-6 tokens/sec inference for 14B-parameter models at 220W peak power; (2) A bidirectional cognitive architecture that synergizes slow deliberative planning (LLM task planning) with fast reactive control (state estimation, mapping, obstacle avoidance, and motion planning). Validated through preliminary results using our prototype, the system demonstrates reliable task planning and scene understanding in communication-constrained environments, such as sugarcane monitoring, power grid inspection, mine tunnel exploration, and biological observation applications. This work establishes a novel framework for embodied aerial artificial intelligence, bridging the gap between task planning and robotic autonomy in open environments.
- **Summary**: The paper presents a hardware-software co-designed framework for enabling general-purpose aerial intelligent agents (AIAs) empowered by large language models (LLMs). The authors address two main limitations: the computational constraints of running LLMs onboard UAVs and the challenge of integrating deliberative LLM planning with fast reactive control. They propose an edge-optimized computing platform capable of running 14B-parameter models and a bidirectional cognitive architecture inspired by dual-process theory. The system's effectiveness is demonstrated through preliminary results in mission-critical applications like sugarcane monitoring, power grid inspection, mine exploration, and biological observation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a timely and interesting idea: enabling general-purpose tasks on UAVs via onboard LLMs. The claim of being the "first aerial intelligent agent capable of open-world task execution" is a strong one, and hinges on the demonstrated capabilities. The hardware co-design, while relying on off-the-shelf components, offers a practical solution to the power and weight constraints that are unique to aerial robotics. The prompt design framework attempting to integrate LLM planning with lower-level control is a valuable contribution. However, edge computing solutions for robotics in general are already established, and some of the core robotics elements (SLAM, state estimation, etc.) are not particularly novel in isolation. The novelty lies in the specific application, integration, and demonstrated performance.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact is significant. If successful, the proposed approach could revolutionize how UAVs are used in various sectors. The ability to perform complex tasks in communication-constrained or unpredictable environments is highly desirable. Moreover, the work addresses a current trend towards embodied AI and shows the practical potential of integrating large models with robotics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined objectives.
    *   Practical hardware design, balancing performance and efficiency.
    *   A plausible architecture that attempts to combine LLM-based reasoning with reactive control.
    *   Demonstration of preliminary results across multiple application domains showcasing the system's generalization ability to perform task planning and scene understanding in real-world scenarios.
    *   The dual-process prompt design architecture and system design appear well conceived.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The results are preliminary. The paper doesn't include extensive quantitative evaluations for metrics such as success rate, efficiency, or robustness in real-world scenarios.
    *   While the hardware design is practical, the use of off-the-shelf components means the design may not be optimally compact for practical applications of UAVs, and lacks the novelty of a complete co-designed system.
    *   The paper doesn't delve deeply into the prompt engineering, a crucial element for LLM performance. How prompts are adapted for different scenarios and the effect of prompt variations are not discussed thoroughly.
    *   There is some lack of clarity between what would be considered *general purpose* compared to *specifically tailored* to the scenario via prompt construction.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can stimulate further research in onboard LLM deployment for UAVs, hardware-software co-design, and prompt engineering. It could also influence the design of more intelligent and autonomous UAV systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has a clear focus on a relevant and challenging problem. It offers a practical hardware-software architecture and integration strategy that addresses the core constraints in a believable manner. The experiments show preliminary promise in a variety of important scenarios, which is compelling. Despite these strengths, some aspects still require stronger validation. The hardware uses off-the-shelf components and lacks specific optimizations. The paper would greatly benefit from in-depth quantitative evaluations demonstrating robustness, as well as discussion of the prompt engineering nuances involved.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Seeing and Reasoning with Confidence: Supercharging Multimodal LLMs with an Uncertainty-Aware Agentic Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08308v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuo Zhi, Chen Feng, Adam Daneshmend, Mine Orlu, Andreas Demosthenous, Lu Yin, Da Li, Ziquan Liu, Miguel R. D. Rodrigues
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) show promise in tasks like visual question answering (VQA) but still face challenges in multimodal reasoning. Recent works adapt agentic frameworks or chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning to improve performance. However, CoT-based multimodal reasoning often demands costly data annotation and fine-tuning, while agentic approaches relying on external tools risk introducing unreliable output from these tools. In this paper, we propose Seeing and Reasoning with Confidence (SRICE), a training-free multimodal reasoning framework that integrates external vision models with uncertainty quantification (UQ) into an MLLM to address these challenges. Specifically, SRICE guides the inference process by allowing MLLM to autonomously select regions of interest through multi-stage interactions with the help of external tools. We propose to use a conformal prediction-based approach to calibrate the output of external tools and select the optimal tool by estimating the uncertainty of an MLLM's output. Our experiment shows that the average improvement of SRICE over the base MLLM is 4.6% on five datasets and the performance on some datasets even outperforms fine-tuning-based methods, revealing the significance of ensuring reliable tool use in an MLLM agent.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a training-free framework called Seeing and Reasoning with Confidence (SRICE) to enhance the multimodal reasoning capabilities of MLLMs, specifically addressing the challenges of unreliable tool usage. SRICE integrates external vision models (object detection and segmentation) with uncertainty quantification (UQ) to guide the inference process. It enables MLLMs to autonomously select regions of interest through multi-stage interactions. The key contributions include using conformal prediction (CP) to calibrate the output of external tools, uncertainty estimation of MLLM outputs to select the optimal tool, and a multi-stage agentic framework. Experimental results across five datasets demonstrate significant performance improvements compared to the base MLLM and some fine-tuning-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a novel combination of existing techniques to address a practical problem in MLLM reasoning. The application of conformal prediction for tool calibration in an agentic MLLM framework is a valuable contribution, addressing the critical issue of unreliable tool outputs. Combining external tools with uncertainty quantification is novel for the particular use case of MLLMs.

*   **Significance:** The issue of unreliable tool outputs in agentic frameworks is a significant bottleneck in MLLM performance. By providing a method for calibration and selection, SRICE addresses this limitation and improves the trustworthiness of MLLMs. The performance improvements shown are statistically significant and demonstrate the practical value of the framework. Additionally, the framework is training-free, which provides greater flexibility.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a key challenge in MLLM reasoning.
    *   Training-free framework, making it easily adaptable to different models and tools.
    *   Solid experimental results demonstrating consistent performance improvements.
    *   Effective use of conformal prediction for tool calibration.
    *   Clearly articulates the limitations of existing methods and provides a well-reasoned solution.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The improvement over some other techniques is not as stark as it could be, suggesting there might be limitations in the design of this technique. It still lags behind SOTA fine-tuned models.
    *   The experimental setup utilizes relatively simple tools, which raises questions about the scalability and effectiveness of SRICE with more complex and potentially more unreliable tools.
    *   The marginal improvement of adding further tools (in Table 5) may be an indication that MLLM is the bottleneck.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of multimodal reasoning. It provides a practical and effective approach to improve the reliability of MLLMs and potentially lead to more robust and trustworthy AI systems. The findings could influence future research on agentic frameworks and uncertainty quantification in MLLMs.

*   **Score Justification:**
    SRICE improves multimodal reasoning capabilities of MLLMs, addresses a major concern with the reliability of tool use. The novelty stems from combining conformal prediction with uncertainty estimation in an agentic environment. The simplicity and training-free nature make it attractive. The score reflects a solid and significant advance but acknowledges there is room for improvement in scalability and exploration of tool interactions.
